On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against general knowledge.\n- Every finding carries a CWE id and a `file:line`.\n- The \"Skills applied\" list is present.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"f0ce17a5-d38c-4caa-b077-118c105d3ef3","session_id":"cb181e91-5faa-4252-b8db-dc330236df70","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_98fea329b83d4ae7ba224ff9","is_error":false,"content":"# UserAuthentication — password authentication with an audit log\n\nA single-file Java implementation of user authentication: it accepts a username and password,\nverifies them against stored account data, and writes one audit record per attempt to an\napplication log. Successful and unsuccessful attempts are both handled, as are locked, disabled\nand damaged accounts.\n\nRequires **JDK 17 or newer**. No dependencies, no build tool, no framework — plain `javac` and\n`java`.\n\n## Files\n\n| File | Purpose |\n| --- | --- |\n| `UserAuthentication.java` | The implementation, plus a runnable demo/interactive entry point. |\n| `AuthTest.java` | Self-contained test harness (305 checks). `java AuthTest`, exit code 0 on success. |\n| `mutation_test.py` | Optional: injects 31 defects into the implementation and checks the suite catches them. |\n| `README.md` | This file. |\n\n## Compile and run\n\n```bash\n# Compile everything (sources are ASCII-only, so no -encoding flag is needed)\njavac -d out UserAuthentication.java AuthTest.java\n\n# Run the test suite (prints one line per check; exits 1 if any check fails)\njava -cp out AuthTest\n\n# Run the demo: a scripted walk through every outcome, writing auth-demo.log\njava -cp out UserAuthentication\n\n# Run it interactively instead (passwords are read with a hidden prompt when a console exists)\njava -cp out UserAuthentication --interactive\n\n# Run specific attempts. Pair syntax is user:password; the log file and KDF cost are options.\njava -cp out UserAuthentication --script \"alice:winter-Frost-42\" \"alice:typo\" \"carol:x\" --log demo.log --iterations 120000\n```\n\nDemo accounts: `alice / winter-Frost-42`, `bob / correct horse battery staple`, plus `dave`\n(used to show lockout), `erin` (disabled) and `carol` (does not exist).\n\nOptions: `--log <file>` (default `auth-demo.log`), `--iterations <n>` (default `60000`; the\nlibrary default for real use is `120000`), `--interactive`, `--script`.\n\nTo use the class from your own code, drop `UserAuthentication.java` next to your sources:\n\n```java\ntry (UserAuthentication.FileEventLogger auditLog =\n         new UserAuthentication.FileEventLogger(Path.of(\"/var/log/auth.log\"))) {\n    UserAuthentication auth = UserAuthentication.withInMemoryStore(\n        auditLog, UserAuthentication.Config.defaults());\n\n    auth.register(\"alice\", passwordChars);            // once, at signup\n\n    UserAuthentication.Result result =\n        auth.authenticate(username, passwordChars, request.getRemoteAddr());\n    if (result.isSuccess()) {\n        session.start(result.username());\n    } else {\n        response.send(result.userMessage());          // safe to show to the user\n        log.warn(\"login refused: {}\", result.auditReason());   // internal detail, never shown\n    }\n}\n```\n\n## What it does\n\n**Verification.** Passwords are stored only as PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 digests (120 000 rounds by\ndefault) with a 16-byte random salt per account, in the self-describing form\n`pbkdf2-sha256$iterations$base64(salt)$base64(key)`. Because the record carries its own salt and\nround count, a stored hash can be verified as-is and re-hashed later when the policy tightens.\nThe plaintext password is never stored, logged, returned or printed; `Result.toString()` and\n`PasswordHash.toString()` are both redacted.\n\n**Usernames** are NFKC-normalized, trimmed and lowercased, then matched against\n`[a-z0-9._-]{3,64}`. `\" Alice \"` and `\"alice\"` name the same account; anything else (an `@`,\na space, a newline, 65 characters) is refused as `INVALID_INPUT` rather than looked up.\n\n**Outcomes.** `authenticate` returns one of five outcomes:\n\n| Outcome | Meaning | `auditReason()` |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `SUCCESS` | Credentials matched, counters reset | `OK` |\n| `INVALID_CREDENTIALS` | Wrong password, or no such user | `BAD_PASSWORD` / `UNKNOWN_USER` |\n| `ACCOUNT_LOCKED` | Too many recent failures; retry later | `ACCOUNT_LOCKED` |\n| `ACCOUNT_DISABLED` | Account switched off by an operator | `ACCOUNT_DISABLED` |\n| `INVALID_INPUT` | Username or password was not a valid shape | `MALFORMED_USERNAME` / `MALFORMED_PASSWORD` |\n\n`userMessage()` is safe to render: the wording for a wrong password and for an unknown user is\nidentical, so the response never reveals whether an account exists. `auditReason()` is for the\naudit trail and logs only.\n\n**Lockout.** `maxFailures` consecutive failures (default 5) inside `failureWindow` (default 30\nminutes) lock the account for `lockDuration` (default 15 minutes). The failure that trips the lock\nis still reported as `INVALID_CREDENTIALS` — the next attempt is the one that sees\n`ACCOUNT_LOCKED` — so an attacker cannot tell the threshold from the response. A verified login\nresets the counter; a lock also expires on its own. `unlock(username)` lets an operator clear a\nlock, and `setEnabled` switches an account off without deleting it.\n\n**Logging.** Every attempt writes exactly one record through the `EventLogger` seam, whichever\noutcome it produced, plus separate events for registration, a lock tripping, auto-expiry and an\noperator unlock. The format is a single ASCII line, machine-readable and reversible via\n`Event.parseLine`:\n\n```\n2026-09-12T03:29:21.568Z|event=LOGIN_FAILURE|user=dave|outcome=INVALID_CREDENTIALS|reason=BAD_PASSWORD|addr=-|durationMs=29|failures=3|detail=lock tripped\n```\n\n`FileEventLogger` flushes after every line (a crash cannot lose the last attempt) and writes under\na lock, so concurrent logins cannot interleave half-lines. `ConsoleEventLogger` prints,\n`EventLogger.nullLogger()` discards, and your own sink implements the one-method `EventLogger`\ninterface.\n\n## Security properties worth knowing\n\n* **Equal work on every path.** Each `authenticate` call performs exactly one key derivation,\n  including the paths that refuse before checking a credential: unknown user, malformed username,\n  unusable password, damaged stored record, disabled account, locked account. Without that,\n  response time alone reveals which usernames exist. The \"charge\" is a real digest of a placeholder\n  secret, generated when the service is constructed — never a hand-written constant, which is how\n  this mitigation usually fails silently (a pasted base64 blob that the strict decoder rejects\n  turns the equal-work branch into a no-op, and every functional test still passes).\n* **Constant-time comparison.** The derived key is compared with `MessageDigest.isEqual`.\n* **Log injection is impossible.** Every field is forced to printable ASCII with CR, LF and the\n  `|` delimiter replaced and long values truncated, so a username cannot forge or split a record.\n* **Unhashable candidates are rejected, not sanitized.** `PBEKeySpec` converts a `char[]` with a\n  lossy encoder: it drops *trailing* NULs and maps every unpaired surrogate to `'?'`, so\n  `\"pw\"`, `\"pw\\0\"` and `\"p\\ud800\"`/`\"pw?\"` collide onto the same digest — one holder could log in\n  as the other. `isHashablePassword` refuses those forms (interior NULs and well-formed surrogate\n  pairs stay legal). `AuthTest` derives both colliding forms with the raw JCE to show the collision\n  is real before asserting the guard.\n* **Strict record parsing.** Stored hashes are base64-decoded with the strict decoder and then\n  re-encoded and compared, so a field that only decoded because a lenient decoder ignored junk is\n  rejected instead of silently becoming a weaker key.\n* **Defensive copying.** `PasswordHash` copies the salt and key in its constructor and returns\n  copies from its accessors, so a caller wiping its own buffers cannot corrupt a stored credential.\n* **Thread safety.** Account state is guarded per account; the failure counter is not lost when\n  concurrent failures race (verified over 100 trials x 8 threads).\n\n## Verification\n\n`java -cp out AuthTest` runs 305 checks and prints `=== 305 checks, 0 failed ===`. It covers:\n\n* username normalization and rejection, password-shape rejection (empty, trailing NUL, unpaired\n  surrogate, oversized), and the raw-JCE collisions those guards exist to prevent;\n* parsing: correct password succeeds, 11 near-miss passwords all refused, case sensitivity,\n  prefix/suffix candidates refused, unknown user refused;\n* lockout arithmetic: which strike trips the lock, what that strike reports, the right password\n  refused while locked, counter reset by login and by operator, failure-window expiry, automatic\n  unlock after `lockDuration`;\n* the audit trail: one record per attempt with the right type/reason/address, log lines parse back,\n  file logger appends and flushes, credentials and hash material never appear, hostile usernames\n  (`CRLF` + a forged record, embedded `|`, control characters, 200 chars) cannot forge a line;\n* equal-work timing across **five** refusal shapes plus the honest-wrong-password baseline\n  (measured medians 41-43 ms each at 30 000 rounds; each ratio must land in 0.6-1.7). Each shape\n  is timed on its own fixture, because per-account lockout would otherwise short-circuit later\n  samples and fabricate a flattering ratio;\n* concurrency: strike-counter updates under contention, interleaving-free concurrent log writes,\n  concurrent correct logins all succeeding.\n\n`python3 mutation_test.py` then proves those checks are load-bearing: it rebuilds the project 31\ntimes with one deliberate defect each (guard removed, comparison reversed, clone dropped, logging\ncall dropped, reason swapped, `MessageDigest.isEqual` replaced with `Arrays.equals`, ...) and\nreports whether the suite notices.\n\n```\ntotal mutants: 31 | killed: 29 | survived: 2 | harness problems: 0\n```\n\nThe two survivors are reported rather than deleted, because neither defect is observable through\nthe public API at this granularity:\n\n1. `non-constant-time-key-compare` — `Arrays.equals` exits early, but the difference is tens of\n   nanoseconds against a ~40 ms key derivation. No honest assertion can pin it; it would only\n   produce a flaky test.\n2. `username-guard-semantic-noop` — re-normalizing the username in the guard is the same decision,\n   so no test can distinguish it from the original.\n\nA mutant whose anchor does not match exactly once is reported as `NOT-APPLIED` and a mutant that\ndoes not compile as `COMPILE-FAIL`; neither is ever counted as a kill. Re-run a single mutant with\n`python3 mutation_test.py <name-substring>`.\n\n## Limitations and how to extend it\n\n* **Storage is in-memory.** `InMemoryAccountStore` is process-local and lost on restart. The\n  `AccountStore` interface (`find` / `register` / `failuresOf`) is the seam: a JDBC store maps to\n  `username, password_hash, enabled, failed_attempts, locked_until`. Keep parameterized queries —\n  never concatenate a username into SQL.\n* **No session/token layer.** This verifies credentials and logs the attempt; issuing a session\n  cookie or JWT is deliberately out of scope.\n* **Lockout counters live with the account object.** A JDBC store should keep them in columns so\n  they survive a restart and are shared across nodes.\n* **`main` is a demo.** It seeds accounts at startup. Real deployments should call\n  `register(...)` from a signup flow behind authorization.\n* **Timing equalization is per-attempt, not per-server.** Under heavy concurrent load the CPU\n  contention noise floor (roughly a millisecond here) dominates the difference the guard protects;\n  the ratios hold because one derivation is tens of milliseconds. Rate limiting in front of the\n  endpoint is the complementary control.\n* **Iteration count is not self-adapting.** 120 000 PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 rounds is a reasonable 2026\n  floor; `Config` lets you raise it, and existing records keep verifying because they carry their\n  own round count.\n"}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"7ece9997-ff7c-4988-9a7c-9d15a7eb0a40","session_id":"cb181e91-5faa-4252-b8db-dc330236df70","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"7ece9997-ff7c-4988-9a7c-9d15a7eb0a40","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Now let me read the main source files. Actually in the JDK, `PBEKeySpec(char[] password...)` does `internalPassword = password.clone()`, and `clearPassword()` sets internalPassword = null (doesn't erase contents). So the password copy remains in memory until GC. More important: the caller's char[] is never cleared by the service — authenticate() never zeroes the caller's password array. The README says register should be behind authorization. Medium/low.\n\n6. **User enumeration via `ACCOUNT_DISABLED` / `ACCOUNT_LOCKED` outcomes** — the userMessage differs for locked and disabled accounts, revealing account existence to unauthenticated attackers. \"Account is not active. Hmm — it's a genuine finding worth reporting: remote-source unbounded attempts → both brute-force throughput and lockout DoS. The `parse` accepts `iterations >= 1000` while policy is 120000; and `authenticate` uses `stored.deriveKey(password)` which uses stored iterations. Also `Config` allows iterations >= 1000, which is below OWASP recommendation (PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 should be ≥600,000 per OWASP 2023+; 120k is the code's own claim for 2026). Hmm: OWASP Password Storage Cheat Sheet 2024 recommends PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 at least 600,000 iterations. The code uses 120,000 as default. That is below current OWASP guidance → CWE-916 (Use of Password Hash With Insufficient Computational Effort). Given the date is 2026 and OWASP recommends 600k for PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, reporting the iteration count as a Low/Medium finding is defensible. It's a demo entry point, but the credentials are real passwords registered into a live service by the shipped `main`. This is a real finding: register() only checks `isHashablePassword` (non-empty, ≤1024, no trailing NUL, no unpaired surrogate). No minimum length or strength check. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- usage for security purposes is flagged\n- [ ] ECB mode usage is flagged\n- [ ] Hardcoded keys/passwords are detected\n- [ ] Weak KDF parameters are identified\n- [ ] Report includes severity, Executive Summary\n\nThe component is a single-file, self-contained password authentication service (`UserAuthentication.java`, 1069 lines) that registers accounts, verifies credentials with PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, enforces per-account lockout, and writes a sanitized pipe-delimited audit log. It is unusually careful for a demo: salts come from `SecureRandom`, the comparison uses `MessageDigest.isEqual`, every authentication path pays exactly one key derivation so an unknown user costs the same as a known one, untrusted log fields are sanitized against CRLF/`|` injection, and `toString()` on the credential-bearing types is redacted. A companion suite (`AuthTest.java`) and a mutation-testing harness (`mutation_test.py`) back that up, and 29 of 31 injected mutants were killed.\n\nThe residual risk is concentrated at the trust boundary around **stored, self-describing KDF records** and in the **CLI demo entry point**, not in the crypto core:\n\n- **One High finding.** `PasswordHash.parse()` accepts any iteration count above a 1 000 floor straight out of the stored record, and `deriveKey()` then runs the KDF with *that* count during login. A record with `pbkdf2-sha256$2147483647$...` makes that account's login hang for effectively forever, and because the derivation happens before the lockout check, an attacker who can write to the account store can turn any single account (or every account) into a permanent denial of service and pin CPU across the thread pool. Nothing caps, normalises, or rejects out-of-policy cost at parse time.\n- **Six Medium findings.** Credentials are accepted on the command line (`--script user:password`), where they are visible in `ps`/`/proc/<pid>/cmdline` and shell history; neither the caller's password `char[]` nor the derived key `byte[]` is ever zeroized, and the no-console interactive fallback routes the password through an immutable `String`; the audit log is created with default permissions and no `PosixFilePermission`/`umask` handling; `FileEventLogger.log()` rethrows an `UncheckedIOException` from inside `authenticate()` *after* the credential check has already been decided, so a full or unwritable log either wedges authentication or silently loses the record depending on how the caller handles the exception; `register()` returns a boolean and logs `created`/`duplicate`, and a public `accountExists()` exists, giving a live user-enumeration oracle; and there is no per-source rate limit, so the per-account lockout is itself an abuse primitive (targeted lock-out of any known username) and a username spray is unthrottled.\n- **Four Low findings.** Hardcoded demo credentials that are also printed to stdout; an unrestricted `--log` path argument that appends to any writable location; a KDF policy floor of 1 000 rounds that formally permits near-unstyled hashing; and no password-strength policy at all — `register()` accepts a single-character password.\n\nNone of the findings require redesigning the hashing scheme. The High one is closed by a policy ceiling at parse time plus re-derivation on the configured cost; the Medium ones are closed by moving credentials off `argv`, wiping buffers in a `finally`, applying restrictive file attributes, decoupling audit-write failure from the authentication result, and adding a source-keyed limiter.\n\n## 2. Scope\n\nReviewed in place: `/data/results-cyber/qwen/set_a/ruth/6/basic/`.\n\n| File | Purpose | Security-relevant behaviour |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| `UserAuthentication.java` (1069 lines) | Production code: account registry, PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 password hashing, per-account lockout, audit logger, CLI demo. | KDF parameters and the self-describing record `pbkdf2-sha256$iterations$base64(salt)$base64(key)` (`665`–`789`); iteration policy floor with no ceiling (`139`–`141`, `675`–`677`); unbounded `iterations` parsed from stored data (`709`–`717`) and used for the login derivation (`750`–`753`); `MessageDigest.isEqual` comparison (`755`–`757`); equal-work derivation on every path (`474`–`478`, `117`); NFKC username normalisation (`594`–`612`); password-form collision guard `isHashablePassword` (`623`–`641`); log-field sanitizer (`645`–`660`); duplicate-username boolean and audit detail (`439`–`454`); public `accountExists` (`559`–`562`); lockout arithmetic (`892`–`901`); audit-file creation, permissions and error handling (`370`–`399`); plaintext credential lifetimes (`759`–`772`, `1013`–`1019`); CLI handling of `--log`, `--iterations`, `--script`, demo passwords (`947`–`1067`). |\n| `AuthTest.java` (1110 lines) | Test harness for the above (38 test groups; writes only to `Files.createTempFile`, deletes afterwards; `System.exit` at `88`). | Exercises the sanitizer, timing equal-work, lockout, corrupt-record handling. Contains a hardcoded test password (`42`). No production sink, no persistence outside temp files. |\n| `mutation_test.py` | Mutation harness: copies the sources into `tempfile.mkdtemp()`, injects 31 mutants, recompiles with `javac` and runs `AuthTest`. | `subprocess` invoked with list arguments — no `shell=True`, no string interpolation into a shell; mutates only copies; `shutil.rmtree(..., ignore_errors=True)` cleanup. Its two surviving mutants are evidence for §6. |\n| `README.md` | Design notes and stated limitations. | Documents the equal-work design, the 120 000-round floor, that `main` is a demo, and that front-end rate limiting is the complementary control. Not executable. |\n\n`prompt.txt`, `cyber_prompt.txt`, `prompt_hash.txt`, `result.md`, `return.txt`, `mut.log`, `out-test.log` and `out/` (compiled `.class` output) are task metadata and build artefacts, not source under review.\n\n## 3. Skills Applied\n\n- `performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application`: the crypto core — `PasswordHash` record format, `derive()`/`generate()`/`parse()` (`665`–`789`), `SecureRandom` salt generation (`689`–`692`), `MessageDigest.isEqual` (`756`), and the KDF cost policy. Its \"check key derivation function parameters\", \"validate random number generator usage\" and \"hardcoded keys/passwords/secrets in source\" objectives produced SEC-01, SEC-10 and SEC-08; its \"Weak KDF / low iteration PBKDF2\" risk row is what the 1 000-round floor is judged against.\n- `testing-for-sensitive-data-exposure`: credential exposure outside the intended channel — `argv` (`1028`–`1040`), non-`Console` interactive fallback (`1013`–`1019`), uncleared `char[]`/`byte[]` buffers (`759`–`772`), the printed demo credentials (`964`–`965`), and the audit file's on-disk permissions (`376`–`381`). Its masking/redaction step was used to check `Result.toString()` and `PasswordHash.toString()` (both redacted — see §6).\n- `testing-api-authentication-weaknesses`: the authentication surface itself — `authenticate()` (`465`–`531`), `register()` (`439`–`454`), the account-enumeration differential test, weak-password acceptance, and lockout behaviour. Drove SEC-06, SEC-07 and SEC-11.\n- `performing-directory-traversal-testing`: the two CLI sinks that accept a caller-supplied path/argument — `pathArgument(args, \"--log\", ...)` (`950`, `1065`–`1067`) and the `argument()` scanner (`1056`–`1063`). Drove SEC-09.\n- No skill in the library covers log-injection hardening, audit-file permission assignment, secure zeroization of credential buffers, denial of service / resource exhaustion, login-attempt restriction by source, or audit-write failure handling. Those behaviours were reviewed against general **OWASP Top 10 (A02 Cryptographic Failures, A05 Security Misconfiguration, A07 Identification and Authentication Failures, A09 Logging Failures)** and **CWE (CWE-400/770, CWE-307, CWE-316/244, CWE-732, CWE-754/248, CWE-117)** knowledge; they account for SEC-01, SEC-02, SEC-03, SEC-04, SEC-05 and SEC-07, and the log-injection control is verified in §6.\n\n## 4. Summary of Findings\n\n| ID | Title | Severity | CWE | Location |\n| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |\n| SEC-01 | Login KDF cost taken from the stored record with no upper bound → account-level denial of service | High | CWE-400 | `UserAuthentication.java:709-717`, `750-753` |\n| SEC-02 | Credentials accepted on the command line (`--script user:password`) | Medium | CWE-214 | `UserAuthentication.java:1028-1040` |\n| SEC-03 | Password `char[]` and derived key `byte[]` never zeroized; interactive fallback puts the password in a `String` | Medium | CWE-316 | `UserAuthentication.java:759-772`, `1013-1019` |\n| SEC-04 | Audit log created with default permissions, no file-mode control | Medium | CWE-732 | `UserAuthentication.java:376-381` |\n| SEC-05 | Audit-write failure rethrown from inside `authenticate()` after the credential decision | Medium | CWE-754 | `UserAuthentication.java:388-399` |\n| SEC-06 | Account-enumeration oracle via `register()` result, audit detail and public `accountExists()` | Medium | CWE-204 | `UserAuthentication.java:439-454`, `559-562` |\n| SEC-07 | No per-source attempt limit; per-account lockout is itself abusable | Medium | CWE-307 | `UserAuthentication.java:465-531`, `892-901` |\n| SEC-08 | Hardcoded demo credentials in source, also printed to stdout | Low | CWE-798 | `UserAuthentication.java:958-965` |\n| SEC-09 | Unrestricted `--log` path is opened for append at any writable location | Low | CWE-22 | `UserAuthentication.java:950`, `1065-1067` |\n| SEC-10 | KDF policy floor of 1 000 rounds permits near-unstyled hashing | Low | CWE-916 | `UserAuthentication.java:139-141`, `675-677` |\n| SEC-11 | No password-strength policy at registration | Low | CWE-521 | `UserAuthentication.java:439-448`, `623-641` |\n\n## 5. Detailed Findings\n\n### SEC-01: Login KDF cost is taken from the unbounded stored record, enabling account-level denial of service\n\n- **Severity:** High\n- **CWE:** CWE-400: Uncontrolled Resource Consumption ('Improper Resource Limitation or Throttling'); also CWE-770: Allocation of Resources Without Limits or Throttling\n- **Location:** `UserAuthentication.java:709-717` (unbounded parse), `UserAuthentication.java:750-753` (that count drives the login derivation)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:709-717\n            int rounds;\n            try {\n                rounds = Integer.parseInt(parts[1]);\n            } catch (NumberFormatException notNumeric) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations is not a number\", notNumeric);\n            }\n            byte[] salt = strictBase64(parts[2]);\n            byte[] key = strictBase64(parts[3]);\n            return new PasswordHash(rounds, salt, key);\n```\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:750-753\n        /** Runs the KDF; the caller owns the returned array. */\n        byte[] deriveKey(char[] password) {\n            return derive(password, salt, iterations);\n        }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** The record is self-describing by design — that is what lets the cost be raised later without a migration. But nothing bounds the value read back out. `PasswordHash`'s constructor only enforces a *floor* (`675`–`677`, `iterations < 1_000`), so `pbkdf2-sha256$2000000000$AAAA...$BBBB...` parses cleanly and becomes a legitimate `Account`. `deriveKey()` then runs PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 with the record's own count, and `authenticate()` calls it at `477` — **before** the enabled check (`496`), **before** the lockout check (`502`) and before any per-source throttle exists. Consequences: (a) anyone able to write to the account store — a compromised persistence layer, an unsafe import/repair path, `Account.withCorruptHash`-adjacent tooling at `842` — can make a single account's login never return, or make every account's login consume minutes-to-hours of CPU each, saturating the request pool while the service reports \"up\"; (b) `new Config(Integer.MAX_VALUE, ...)` is legal, so a single operator mistake at startup is a permanent outage; (c) because the derivation is deliberately equal-work on the unknown-user path too, the cheapest possible unauthenticated probe still triggers a KDF, so an attacker spraying usernames amplifies the cost rather than being cheaply rejected. The `--iterations` CLI value (`951`) is parsed with `Integer.parseInt` and flows straight into `Config`, so the demo path exposes the same lack of a ceiling. The README calls 120 000 rounds \"a reasonable 2026 floor\", which is exactly the shape of the bug: a floor with no ceiling is not a policy.\n\n**Fix:** Treat the iteration count in a stored record as hostile input and clamp it to an accepted band at parse time, rejecting anything above the current policy maximum (and, separately, migrating records that fall below today's floor rather than honouring them). Then derive with a count the service has chosen, not one the data chose:\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:709-717 (corrected)\n            int rounds;\n            try {\n                // Long.parseLong first so that \"99999999999999\" is a policy violation\n                // rather than a silent NumberFormatException-turned-400.\n                long parsed = Long.parseLong(parts[1]);\n                if (parsed < MAX_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS_LONG) {\n                    throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations above policy maximum\");\n                }\n                rounds = (int) parsed;\n            } catch (NumberFormatException notNumeric) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations is not a number\", notNumeric);\n            }\n            byte[] salt = strictBase64(parts[2]);\n            byte[] key = strictBase64(parts[3]);\n            // Never exceed the deployment's policy ceiling, whatever the record claims.\n            return new PasswordHash(Math.min(rounds, config.maxIterations()), salt, key);\n```\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:675-677 (corrected) — bound both ends, in one place\n            if (iterations < MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS || iterations > MAX_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations must be in [\"\n                    + MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS + \", \" + MAX_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS + \"], got \" + iterations);\n            }\n```\n\nAdditionally: keep a per-attempt wall-clock budget (`deriveKey` on a dedicated, bounded executor with a timeout that fails closed), reject `--iterations` values outside the band at `951` instead of trusting `Integer.parseInt`, and upgrade any record whose count is below the current floor on successful login so cost only ever moves up.\n\n### SEC-02: Credentials passed on the command line are visible to other local principals\n\n- **Severity:** Medium\n- **CWE:** CWE-214: Exposure of Sensitive Information in an OS Command's Arguments (also CWE-522)\n- **Location:** `UserAuthentication.java:1028-1040` (`scripted`), reached from `UserAuthentication.java:972-974`\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:1028-1040\n    private static void scripted(UserAuthentication auth, String[] args, PrintStream out) {\n        for (String pair : args) {\n            // Usernames can never contain ':', so splitting at the last one lets the password do so.\n            int at = pair.lastIndexOf(':');\n            if (at < 0 || pair.startsWith(\"--\")) {\n                continue;\n            }\n            show(out, pair.substring(0, at),\n                auth.authenticate(pair.substring(0, at), pair.substring(at + 1).toCharArray(), \"cli\"));\n        }\n    }\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** Any credential handed to `java UserAuthentication --script alice:winter-Frost-42` is a plaintext secret in the process argument vector for the lifetime of the JVM. On Linux every local user can read `/proc/<pid>/cmdline` (and `ps -ef`, `ps auxww`) for a process they do not own; `argv` is also written into shell history (`~/.bash_history`), into `auditd`/`sysmon` exec telemetry, into CI job logs and container-inspect output, and into crash/hang dumps. A shared build agent or a multi-tenant host running this demo therefore leaks every scripted password to every other account on the box, and the passwords leak again into whatever log shipper collects the CI output. This is also the only path that lets a credential reach the service *without* the `char[]`-erasure affordances the API otherwise provides — the secret is unavoidably an immutable `String` taken straight out of `args`.\n\n**Fix:** Take scripted credentials from stdin or from a descriptor/file with restrictive permissions, never from `argv`, and refuse to start if the argument looks like a credential:\n\n```java\n// corrected: read \"user:password\" pairs from stdin, one per line\n    private static void scripted(UserAuthentication auth, String[] args, PrintStream out)\n            throws IOException {\n        BufferedReader in = new BufferedReader(new java.io.InputStreamReader(\n            System.in, StandardCharsets.UTF_8));\n        String pair;\n        while ((pair = in.readLine()) != null) {\n            int at = pair.lastIndexOf(':');\n            if (at < 0) {\n                continue;\n            }\n            char[] password = pair.substring(at + 1).toCharArray();\n            try {\n                show(out, pair.substring(0, at),\n                    auth.authenticate(pair.substring(0, at), password, \"stdin\"));\n            } finally {\n                java.util.Arrays.fill(password, '\\0');   // erase as soon as the call returns\n            }\n        }\n    }\n```\n\n`main()` should then reject any `args` element matching `[^-][^:]*:` with a message pointing at stdin, so an operator who types the old form is told rather than silently leaking. (For scripted pairs that must come from a file, open it via a `--script-file` path and check the mode is no wider than `0600` first.)\n\n### SEC-03: Cleartext credential buffers are never erased; the no-console fallback routes the password through a `String`\n\n- **Severity:** Medium\n- **CWE:** CWE-316: Cleartext Storage of Sensitive Information in a Memory Region (also CWE-244: Improper Clearing of Memory Before Release)\n- **Location:** `UserAuthentication.java:759-772` (`derive`), `UserAuthentication.java:1013-1019` (`interactive` fallback)\n\n**Vulnerable code:**\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:759-772\n        private static byte[] derive(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterations) {\n            if (!isHashablePassword(password)) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password form cannot be hashed safely\");\n            }\n            PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(password, salt, iterations, KEY_BYTES * 8);\n            try {\n                SecretKeyFactory factory = SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(KDF);\n                return factory.generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n            } catch (GeneralSecurityException unsupported) {\n                throw new IllegalStateException(KDF + \" is unavailable on this JVM\", unsupported);\n            } finally {\n                spec.clearPassword();\n            }\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:1013-1019\n                out.print(\"username: \");\n                out.flush();\n                username = in.readLine();\n                out.print(\"password: \");\n                out.flush();\n                String line = in.readLine();\n                password = line == null ? null : line.toCharArray();\n```\n\n**Why it is a problem:** `spec.clearPassword()` erases only the copy that `PBEKeySpec` made internally. Two buffers that the API exists to make erasable are left behind: the caller's `password` `char[]`, which is passed in at `759` and never zeroized by `derive`, `deriveKey`, `authenticate` or any `register` path (a search of the file for zeroization finds none — `Arrays.fill` appears nowhere), and the derived `byte[]` returned at `766`, whose javadoc at `750` explicitly says \"the caller owns the returned array\" while `authenticate()` (`477`) never writes to it again after `stored.matches(presented)` (`514`). Both therefore stay resident in the heap until the garbage collector happens to move and reuse those pages, and are dumped wholesale by a heap dump, a core dump, `jmap`, a crash reporter, or a memory-scraping attacker on a shared host. The point of the `char[]` API — over `String` — is precisely that it can be erased; not erasing it forfeits the benefit while keeping the cost. The fallback at `1018` is worse: when `System.console()` is null (any non-tty invocation: CI, pipe, IDE run, `systemd` service), the password is read via `BufferedReader.readLine()` into an **immutable `String`** that is neither erasable nor guaranteed to leave the heap, and is additionally echoed to the terminal because `readLine` cannot suppress input without a `Console`. That silently downgrades security based on how the process was launched, with no warning.\n\n**Fix:** Erase on every exit path in a `finally`, including the returned key, and refuse to accept a credential that has already become a `String`:\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:759-772 (corrected)\n        private static byte[] derive(char[] password, byte[] salt, int iterations) {\n            if (!isHashablePassword(password)) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password form cannot be hashed safely\");\n            }\n            byte[] key = null;\n            PBEKeySpec spec = new PBEKeySpec(password, salt, iterations, KEY_BYTES * 8);\n            try {\n                SecretKeyFactory factory = SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(KDF);\n                key = factory.generateSecret(spec).getEncoded();\n                return key;\n            } catch (GeneralSecurityException unsupported) {\n                throw new IllegalStateException(KDF + \" is unavailable on this JVM\", unsupported);\n            } finally {\n                spec.clearPassword();\n                java.util.Arrays.fill(password, '\\0');   // caller's buffer: erase before returning\n            }\n        }\n```\n\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:1013-1019 (corrected)\n            } else {\n                // No Console means no echo-free read and no way to avoid an immutable String.\n                // Fail closed rather than downgrade silently.\n                throw new IOException(\"no console available: refusing to read a password \"\n                    + \"from a non-tty stdin; run with --script and feed credentials on stdin\");\n            }\n```\n\n`authenticate()` should likewise zeroize `presented` once the comparison returns (`java.util.Arrays.fill(presented, (byte) 0)` in a `finally` around the `matches` call at `514`), and the contract comment at `750` should state that ownership means an obligation to erase.\n"}}],"stop_reason":"tool_use","usage":{"input_tokens":142252,"output_tokens":8627,"cache_read_input_tokens":0,"total_tokens":150879}}},{"type":"user","uuid":"54c8fa00-bbaa-45ac-aa27-1f3cf23369f7","session_id":"cb181e91-5faa-4252-b8db-dc330236df70","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_c44437b05f584d7d8e9bbb7a","is_error":false,"content":"Successfully created and wrote to new file: /data/results-cyber/qwen/set_a/ruth/6/basic/security_review.md."}]}},{"type":"assistant","uuid":"5ce42df2-1b84-4a58-a185-280b49e15343","session_id":"cb181e91-5faa-4252-b8db-dc330236df70","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"id":"5ce42df2-1b84-4a58-a185-280b49e15343","type":"message","role":"assistant","model":"vllm-general","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Wait — I need to check ordering by severity: High first, then Medium, then Low. Alternative: do the tail append first (biggest), then two small corrections.\n\nLet me now compose the SEC-04 … §6 text carefully.\n\n---\n\n### SEC-04: Audit log is created with default (typically world-readable) permissions\n\n- Severity: **Medium**\n- CWE: CWE-732: Incorrect Permission Assignment for Critical Resource\n- Location: `UserAuthentication.java:376-381` (`FileEventLogger` constructor)\n\nVulnerable code (376-381):\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:376-381\n            try {\n                if (file.getParent() != null) {\n                    Files.createDirectories(file.getParent());\n                }\n                this.writer = Files.newBufferedWriter(file, StandardCharsets.US_ASCII,\n                    StandardOpenOption.CREATE, StandardOpenOption.WRITE, StandardOpenOption.APPEND);\n```\n\nWhy: The audit file records every login attempt — usernames, source addresses, outcomes. It is created with `Files.newBufferedWriter`, which applies the JVM umask default (typically 0644 → readable by every local account), and the parent directory is created with `Files.createDirectories`, which uses the default 0777&~umask. On a shared or multi-tenant host, any local user can read the trail: which accounts exist, which are being attacked, from where, and which accounts an attacker already unlocked — a roadmap for spraying exactly the accounts that are not yet locked. The log also cannot be trusted as evidence, because other non-root users may be able to write or truncate it if the directory is group/world writable. This is the classic \"log file is the crown jewel\" case: the file that records the attacks is the one file nobody protects. OWASP ASVS V7 / CWE-732; also relevant to compliance evidence (audit data must be protected from unauthorized modification).\n\nFix: set the mode on creation via PosixFilePermissions, and tighten an already-existing file before appending. Also create the parent with an owner-only mode.\n\n```java\n// corrected\n        private static final Set<PosixFilePermission> OWNER_ONLY =\n            PosixFilePermissions.fromString(\"rw-------\");\n\n        public FileEventLogger(Path file, PrintStream echo) {\n            if (file == null) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"file is required\");\n            }\n            this.file = file;\n            this.echo = echo;\n            try {\n                if (file.getParent() != null) {\n                    Files.createDirectories(file.getParent(),\n                        PosixFilePermissions.asFileAttribute(\n                            PosixFilePermissions.fromString(\"rwx------\")));\n                }\n                this.writer = Files.newBufferedWriter(file, StandardCharsets.US_ASCII,\n                    PosixFilePermissions.asFileAttribute(OWNER_ONLY),\n                    StandardOpenOption.CREATE, StandardOpenOption.WRITE, StandardOpenOption.APPEND);\n                if (!Files.getPosixFilePermissions(file).equals(OWNER_ONLY)) {\n                    Files.setPosixFilePermissions(file, OWNER_ONLY);   // pre-existing file\n                }\n            } catch (IOException cannotOpen) {\n                throw new UncheckedIOException(\"cannot open audit log \" + file, cannotOpen);\n            }\n        }\n```\n(with `import java.nio.file.attribute.PosixFilePermission/PosixFilePermissions; import java.util.Set;`; on non-POSIX filesystems the attribute is ignored, so the ACL equivalent must be applied there — e.g. `icacls` on Windows.)\n\n### SEC-05: Audit failure throws mid-authentication, aborting the request with an unchecked exception\n\n- Severity: Medium\n- CWE: CWE-754: Improper Check for Unusual or Exceptional Conditions (unchecked exception escaping a request path, CWE-248)\n- Location: `UserAuthentication.java:388-399` (`FileEventLogger.log`), reached from `UserAuthentication.java:482-483`, `518-521`, `529-531`\n\nVulnerable code:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:388-399\n        public void log(Event event) {\n            String line = event.format();\n            synchronized (lock) {\n                try {\n                    writer.write(line);\n                    newLine...\n```\nexact:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:388-399\n        public void log(Event event) {\n            String line = event.format();\n            synchronized (lock) {\n                try {\n                    writer.write(line);\n                    writer.newLine();\n                    writer.flush();\n                } catch (IOException writeFailed) {\n                    throw new UncheckedIOException(\"cannot write audit log \" + file, writeFailed);\n                }\n            }\n        }\n```\nWait 399 is the closing brace of synchronized, 400-402 echo + method close. Range 388-399 covers through `}` of synchronized; the method ends at 402. I'll use 388-402 to be complete.\n\nWhy: An audit trail that can only be written is a control-plane dependency of the login path, and here its failure aborts the login with a runtime exception thrown out of `authenticate()`. Two consequences:\n1. Availability / fail-open vs fail-closed is accidental. When the disk fills, the log is rotated by an external tool that unlinks the file, or the file hits a size limit on a filesystem without append-after-unlink, every subsequent `authenticate()` call dies with `UncheckedIOException` before returning a `Result` — the caller sees a stack trace instead of a login decision, and login is down because of a logging problem. Nothing catches it in this component: `main` only catches `IOException` around CLI setup, so an exception on line 518's path escapes `main` and kills the process.\n2. The failure is itself unauditable — the security event you most need (audit log stopped writing) cannot be recorded, and no operator is alerted; the only trace is an exception on whoever's thread happened to trigger it.\nThe `synchronized (lock)` also means every login thread serialises behind the disk write, so a slow/stalled device (NFS hard mount) turns into a login stall for the whole service — a resource-exhaustion amplifier (CWE-400) on top of the error-handling problem.\n\nFix: separate the two decisions. `log()` should never throw at the caller; it records the failure, alerts, and tells the caller whether the record landed. Policy then decides: authentication decisions are still returned to the user, but a *successful* login whose audit record could not be persisted is downgraded to a refusal (fail closed on the sensitive record), and an `AUDIT_ERROR` condition is surfaced to the operator out-of-band.\n```java\n// corrected\n        /** @return true when the record is durably handed to the OS; never throws to callers. */\n        public boolean log(Event event) {\n            String line = event.format();\n            boolean written;\n            synchronized (lock) {\n                try {\n                    writer.write(line);\n                    writer.newLine();\n                    writer.flush();\n                    written = true;\n                } catch (IOException | RuntimeException writeFailed) {\n                    written = false;\n                    lastError = writeFailed;                       // kept for the health endpoint\n                    System.err.println(\"AUDIT DOWN: cannot write \" + file + \": \" + writeFailed);\n                }\n            }\n            if (written && echo != null) {\n                echo.println(line);\n            }\n            return written;\n        }\n```\nand in `authenticate`, only the success path depends on it:\n```java\n// corrected, in authenticate(), replacing the unconditional log.log(...) before returning\n        if (success) {\n            if (!log.log(new Event(EventType.LOGIN_SUCCESS, username, remoteAddress,\n                Reason.OK, null, attempts, cleared, \"ok\"))) {\n                return new Result(Outcome.FAILURE, Reason.INTERNAL_ERROR, username,\n                    \"Login could not be completed.\", elapsedMillis(startedAt), attempts);\n            }\n        } else {\n            log.log(new Event(EventType.LOGIN_FAILURE, username, remoteAddress,\n                reason, null, attempts, 0, \"refused\"));\n        }\n        return new Result(Outcome.SUCCESS, ...);\n```\nplus a monitoring hook / alert on `lastError`, and (separately) an async or ring-buffered writer so the disk write is off the authentication critical path.\n\nHmm — careful: the corrected success flow must not return SUCCESS twice. Let me write it cleanly:\n\n```java\n        // corrected tail of authenticate()\n        long millis = elapsedMillis(startedAt);\n        if (!success) {\n            log.log(new Event(EventType.LOGIN_FAILURE, username, remoteAddress, reason,\n                null, attempts, 0, \"refused\"));\n            return new Result(Outcome.FAILURE, reason, username, elapsedMillis(startedAt), attempts);\n        }\n        if (!log.log(new Event(EventType.LOGIN_SUCCESS, username, remoteAddress, Reason.OK,\n            null, attempts, cleared, \"ok\"))) {\n            // No audit record, no declared success: fail closed and alert.\n            return new Result(Outcome.ERROR, Reason.INTERNAL_ERROR, username, millis, attempts);\n        }\n        return new Result(Outcome.SUCCESS, Reason.OK, username, millis, attempts);\n```\nThis uses a `success` boolean from the existing branch structure; mention the restructure needed since success is currently returned from inside branches. Good, and mention `elapsedMillis` helper. Keep prose.\n\n### SEC-06: `register()` and `accountExists()` hand callers a live account-enumeration oracle\n\nSeverity Medium; CWE-204: Observable Response Discrepancy. Location `UserAuthentication.java:439-454`, `UserAuthentication.java:559-562`.\n\nVulnerable:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:439-454 (tail) ... \n```\nI'll quote the whole method 439-454 as excerpt. And second block accountExists 559-562.\n\nHmm, one finding but two code blocks? The template says a `**Vulnerable code:**` fenced block (singular) then why then fix. I'll put both excerpts in the same fenced block separated by a comment line with its own `// UserAuthentication.java:559-562` marker. That keeps one block and both excerpts exact. Good.\n\nWhy: `authenticate()` was deliberately hardened against enumeration — identical user-facing wording (`userMessage`, `231`-`243`), an equal-work digest for unknown users (`474`-`478`), internal-only reasons. `register()` then hands back exactly what that hardening was protecting: a boolean distinguishing \"that username is free\" from \"that username is taken\", plus an audit `detail` of `created`/`duplicate` (`452`). `accountExists()` (`559`-`562`) makes it explicit and cheap — a single normalized lookup with no rate limit, no KDF cost and no lockout consequence. Any application that wires these into a signup form or an admin screen exposes a free oracle for building a list of valid accounts, which is the prerequisite for the targeted guessing and password-spraying that the login path is designed to resist, and it defeats the `equal-work` timing defence entirely (there is no derivation at all on these paths). Note the asymmetry the design already recognises for `setEnabled`/`unlock` (`534`-`556`), which return `Outcome` rather than \"does this account exist\".\n\nFix: don't return existence, and don't let the audit trail become the oracle either. Provide one outcome-blind registration API whose duplicate case is indistinguishable to the caller, and delete the public existence probe (or restrict it to an already-authenticated administrative identity outside this class).\n```java\n    // corrected: registration tells the caller nothing it can use as an oracle\n    public Result register(String rawUsername, char[] password, String remoteAddress) {\n        String username = normalizeUsername(rawUsername);\n        if (username == null || !isHashablePassword(password)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"registration request rejected\");\n        }\n        PasswordHash hash = PasswordHash.generate(password, config.iterations, random);\n        accounts.register(username, hash);                     // created-or-merged, caller cannot tell\n        // Generic detail: the audit trail keeps the truth for responders, the API does not.\n        log.log(new Event(EventType.ACCOUNT_REGISTERED, username, remoteAddress, Reason.OK,\n            null, 0, 0, \"attempted\"));\n        return new Result(Outcome.SUCCESS, Reason.OK, username, 0L, 0);\n    }\n\n    // accountExists() is removed from the public surface; an admin service that needs it\n    // queries the store behind its own authorization check, not through this class.\n```\n\n### SEC-07: The only rate limit is per-account, so both spraying and targeted lockout-DoS are unthrottled\n\nSeverity Medium; CWE-307: Improper Restriction of Excessive Authentication Attempts. Location `UserAuthentication.java:465-533`, `UserAuthentication.java:892-904`.\n\nExcerpt: recordFailure 892-904 quoted. Plus mention authenticate.\n\nWhy: `recordFailure` (`892`-`904`) counts failures *inside one `Account`*, so the only limit is `maxFailures` per username per `failureWindow`. Two ways this fails:\n1. Spray: a single source can try `hunter2`, `hunter2!`, `hunter2123`… against `bob`, then move to `carol`, `dave`, … `alice`. Each username absorbs at most `maxFailures` attempts before it locks, so a list of 10 000 accounts yields 30 000 guesses per window with no per-source cost at all, and every locked account is a bonus. Nothing in the file tracks attempts per `remoteAddress`, per source network, or per second, globally. `remoteAddress` is sanitized and recorded but never read for a decision (grep confirms: it is only ever used to build an `Event`).\n2. Lockout as a weapon: because the threshold is per-account and unauthenticated, an attacker can lock out any known user (or, with the `accountExists` oracle from SEC-06, every user) at will, denying them access while their helpdesk ticket queues. `unlock()` (`546`-`556`) is the only escape and it is unauthenticated here too.\nAggravating cost: `authenticate` performs the PBKDF2 derivation *first* (`477`), before the enabled/lockout/decision checks, so an attacker also buys CPU with every request — 3 concurrent spray threads at `DEFAULT_ITERATIONS` saturate cores (SEC-01 makes it far worse). README acknowledges the gap (\"Rate limiting … complementary control\") but no hook implements it.\n\nFix: add a source-keyed budget that is consulted *before* the KDF, cap lock-out as a defence-in-depth measure but make it per-source rather than per-account so lockout stops being a denial primitive, and make the failure counter cheap (no derivation on already-locked sources).\n```java\n// corrected: cheap per-source budget consulted before any key derivation\n    private final Map<String, SlidingWindow> budgetBySource = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n\n    /** @param cost CPU cost of the attempt, in derivation-equivalents. */\n    private boolean sourceAllowed(String remoteAddress, int cost) {\n        if (remoteAddress == null) {\n            return true;                                   // no address: cannot budget, log loudly\n        }\n        SlidingWindow window = budgetBySource.computeIfAbsent(remoteAddress,\n            key -> new SlidingWindow(config.maxAttemptsPerSource, config.sourceWindow));\n        return window.tryAcquire(cost, config.clock.instant());\n    }\n\n    public Result authenticate(String rawUsername, char[] password, String remoteAddress) {\n        ... normalization ...\n        if (!sourceAllowed(remoteAddress, passwordUsable ? 1 : 0)) {\n            account.recordAttemptWithoutDerivation(now);   // count it, but do not pay for it\n            return refuse(username, Reason.RATE_LIMITED, remoteAddress, startedAt, 0, 0);\n        }\n        ... existing flow: equal-work digest, lockout checks, matches(...) ...\n    }\n```\nand make lockout not a per-account denial primitive — keep `maxFailures` but add progressive delay rather than a hard lock, and require an authenticated operator for `unlock`:\n```java\n// corrected lockout accounting: escalating delay, per-source ceiling, no free DoS\n        synchronized FailureState recordFailure(Instant now, Config config) {\n            if (lastFailure != null\n                && Duration.between(lastFailure, now).compareTo(config.failureWindow) > 0) {\n                failures = 0;\n            }\n            failures++;\n            lastFailure = now;\n            if (failures >= config.maxFailures) {\n                int steps = Math.min(failures - config.maxFailures, MAX_BACKOFF_STEPS);\n                lockedUntil = now.plus(config.lockDuration multiplied? ...\n```\nSimplify: `lockedUntil = now.plus(escalatedLockout(failures, config));` with exponential backoff capped at, say, 24h, plus a config `maxFailuresPerSource`. I'll present a concise version:\n```java\n            if (failures >= config.maxFailures) {\n                long seconds = config.lockDuration.toSeconds()\n                    << Math.min(failures - config.maxFailures, MAX_BACKOFF_STEPS);   // 15m, 30m, 1h…\n                lockedUntil = now.plus(Duration.ofSeconds(Math.min(seconds, MAX_LOCK_SECONDS)));\n            }\n            return new FailureState(failures, failures >= config.maxFailures);\n```\nplus config knobs `maxAttemptsPerSource`, `sourceWindow`; and `unlock`/`setEnabled` guarded by a caller identity check.\n\n### SEC-08: Working credentials are hardcoded in the shipped program and printed on every run\n\nSeverity Low; CWE-798: Use of Hard-coded Credentials (with CWE-522 exposure in stdout). Location `UserAuthentication.java:958-965`, `AuthTest.java:42`.\n\nExcerpt: main 958-965 (register lines + println) + AuthTest.java:42 in same block.\n\nWhy: `main` is described as a demo, but it is the code everyone runs and copies. It plants four live accounts with real, strong-looking passwords in the source (`alice/winter-Frost-42`, `bob/correct horse battery staple`, `dave/hunter2-hunter2`, `erin/sup3r-Secret!`) and then prints two of them to stdout (`964`-`965`) on every run, into terminal scrollback, CI logs, and any captured output. Three concrete harms: (1) if anyone reuses the pattern for a deployment, or copies the demo wiring, the credentials travel with the binary and are trivially recoverable from the source or the class file; (2) the printed line means the credentials are written to whatever log captures stdout, defeating the otherwise careful effort to keep secrets out of logs; (3) `winter-Frost-42` also appears in the test suite as `PW` (`AuthTest.java:42`), so the same string is the project's de-facto \"good password\" and will propagate. The lockout demo also demonstrates `hunter2-hunter2` — a well-known joke password and a near-certain hit for any wordlist+rules spray.\n\nFix: generate the demo secrets at run time from a non-source location, or prompt for them, and never print them. Keep a deterministic value only in the tests, where it can never be confused with a real secret, and derive it from a per-run random value where the test allows it.\n```java\n// corrected\n            SecureRandom rng = new SecureRandom();\n            String alicePassword = randomDemoPassword(rng);            // generated, never committed\n            auth.register(\"alice\", alicePassword.toCharArray());\n            ...\n            out.println(\"Demo account alice was created with a one-time password. It is not printed.\");\n            out.println(\"Re-run with --interactive to log in.\");\n```\n```java\n// corrected helper\n    private static String randomDemoPassword(SecureRandom rng) {\n        final String ALPHABET =\n            \"abcdefghijkmnopqrstuvwxyzABCDEFGHJKLMNPQRSTUVWXYZ23456789!@#$%^&*-_=+\";\n        StringBuilder password = new StringBuilder(20);\n        for (int i = 0; i < 20; i++) {\n            password.append(ALPHABET.charAt(rng.nextInt(ALPHABET.length())));\n        }\n        return password.toString();\n    }\n```\nAnd if the demo must accept a caller-supplied secret, read it from an environment variable or a prompt rather than embedding it (`System.getenv(\"DEMO_PASSWORD\")`, or `console.readPassword()`), and add a CI check that fails on credential-shaped string literals in `main`.\n\n### SEC-09: `--log` accepts an arbitrary path with no confinement\n\nSeverity Low; CWE-22: Improper Limitation of a Pathname… ('Path Traversal'). Location `UserAuthentication.java:950`, `1065-1067`.\n\nExcerpt:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:950\n            Path logFile = pathArgument(args, \"--log\", \"auth-demo.log\");\n// UserAuthentication.java:1065-1067\n    private static Path pathArgument(String[] args, String name, String fallback) {\n        return Path.of(argument(args, name, fallback));\n    }\n```\n\nWhy: The audit destination is taken verbatim from argv: `Path.of(...)` of whatever follows `--log`, with no normalization, no containment check and no rejection of absolute paths or `..` segments. `FileEventLogger` then *creates parent directories* (`377`-`379`) and opens the target with `CREATE, WRITE, APPEND` (`380`-`381`). Anything that lets an untrusted party influence the process arguments — a wrapper script, a job template, a config-management field, a CI parameter, a container entrypoint that interpolates user input — becomes an arbitrary file-write primitive: append attacker-controlled `key=value` lines to `~/.ssh/authorized_keys2`, `.bashrc`, a systemd unit, a cron file, or an application config, and create directories in paths the process can reach. The written content is sanitized (no CR/LF, `|`, ASCII printable, ≤64 chars per field) but the *target* is completely unconstrained, and appending attacker-controlled text to an arbitrary file is exactly the primitive used for the classic `.ssh/rc` / log-format pipes. `--iterations` is at least range-checked (`951`); the path is not checked at all.\n\nFix: treat the argument as a file *name inside a configured audit directory*, resolve it, and refuse anything that escapes:\n```java\n// corrected\n    private static final Path AUDIT_DIR = Path.of(\n        System.getProperty(\"auth.audit.dir\", \"audit\")).toAbsolutePath().normalize();\n\n    private static Path pathArgument(String[] args, String name, String fallback) {\n        Path requested = Path.of(argument(args, name, fallback));\n        if (requested.isAbsolute() || requested.getNameCount() != 1) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(name + \" must be a file name inside \" + AUDIT_DIR);\n        }\n        Path resolved = AUDIT_DIR.resolve(requested).normalize();\n        if (!resolved.startsWith(AUDIT_DIR) || !resolved.getFileName().equals(requested)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(name + \" escapes the audit directory\");\n        }\n        return resolved;\n    }\n```\nplus keep SEC-04's owner-only mode, and (for a real service) resolve the destination from trusted server configuration rather than from argv at all.\n\n### SEC-10: Both KDF cost floors are 1 000 iterations, two orders of magnitude below the project's own default\n\nSeverity Low; CWE-916: Use of Password Hash With Insufficient Computational Effort. Location `UserAuthentication.java:139-141`, `UserAuthentication.java:674-677`.\n\nExcerpt:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:674-677\n        PasswordHash(int iterations, byte[] salt, byte[] key) {\n            if (iterations < 1_000) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations must be >= 1000, got \" + iterations);\n            }\n```\nand Config 137-141 in the same block:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:139-141\n            if (iterations < 1_000) {\n                throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"iterations must be >= 1000, got \" + iterations);\n            }\n```\nBoth identical strings — need to distinguish: quote Config's at 139-141 and PasswordHash's at 675-677 (with the signature line 674). Fine as two sub-blocks.\n\nWhy: `Config` defaults to `DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 120_000` (`89`) and README states \"PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256, 120 000 iterations (2026 floor)\", but nothing in the type system or the loader enforces that number: the only gate is `iterations < 1_000`, on either the construction config (`139`) or a stored record's count (`675`). A record at `pbkdf2-sha256$1000$…` is accepted and verified happily, and any caller that constructs `Config` with 1 000 iterations (a copy-paste from a tutorial, a test constant like `FAST_ITERATIONS = 2_000` leaking into production, a config-file typo `1200` for `120000`) silently drops the whole user base to ~1 000 rounds. Against offline cracking of a stolen store, PBKDF2 cost is *the* defence: 1 000 iterations on a modern GPU is millions of guesses per second per key, so the difference between the documented 120 000 and a permitted 1 000 is ~120× less work for the attacker, before accounting for SEC-01's unbounded ceiling. The floor is also policy-expressible rather than tool-expressible: it should encode \"acceptable in 2026\", not \"not obviously absurd\".\n\nFix: make the *minimum* the security policy, with an explicit override for tests, and warn/reject below a floor aligned with the documented default. Keep the constant in one place so config and record validation cannot drift apart.\n```java\n// corrected\n    /** Offline-cracking floor: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 below this is not an acceptable cost. */\n    public static final int MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS = 100_000;\n    public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 120_000;\n\n    /** Test hook: only package-private, so production callers cannot lower the floor. */\n    static final int TEST_ITERATIONS_FLOOR = 1_000;\n    private static volatile int iterationFloor = MIN_ACCEPTED_ITERATIONS;\n\n    static void requireAcceptableIterations(int iterations) {\n        if (iterations < iterationFloor) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"PBKDF2 iterations \" + iterations\n                + \" is below the accepted minimum \" + iterationFloor);\n        }\n    }\n```\n```java\n// corrected: Config and PasswordHash both call the same policy\n        Config(int iterations, int maxFailures, Duration lockDuration, Duration failureWindow,\n            Clock clock) {\n            requireAcceptableIterations(iterations);\n            ...\n        }\n```\n```java\n// corrected: same check on a parsed/stored record\n        PasswordHash(int iterations, byte[] salt, byte[] key) {\n            requireAcceptableIterations(iterations);\n            ...\n        }\n```\nand, rather than letting tests lower a global, give `AuthTest`/`mutation_test.py` an explicit, clearly-labelled constructor (e.g. `Config.forTesting(...)`) that documents the weakened cost and refuses to be used outside a test build (`if (!IS_TEST_BUILD) throw …`).\n\n### SEC-11: No password strength policy — the registration gate is shape-only\n\nSeverity Low; CWE-521: Weak Password Requirements. Location `UserAuthentication.java:445-448` (the only gate, via `623`-`642`).\n\nExcerpt:\n```java\n// UserAuthentication.java:445-448\n        if (!isHashablePassword(password)) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password is empty, too long, ends with NUL or \"\n                + \"contains an unpaired surrogate\");\n        }\n```\n\nWhy: `register()` validates only that a secret is hashable — non-empty, ≤ 1024 chars, no trailing NUL, no unpaired surrogate (`623`-`642`). `auth.register(\"mallory\", \"a\".toCharArray())` succeeds, as does `\"password\"`, `\"12345678\"`, `\"qwertyuiop\"`. Everything the rest of the design does to slow guessing — 120 000 rounds, a 3-strike lockout, an equal-work digest for unknown users — is nullified the moment the stored secret is a 4-letter word, because PBKDF2 cost protects against *offline* cracking only; online guessing against a weak password succeeds at attempt one, and a store full of weak passwords is a store that can be cracked offline regardless of the iteration count. This component also ships the counter-example itself: `dave` is registered as `hunter2-hunter2` (`960`), which is exactly the wordlist+double-the-word rule a spray tool implements by default. There is no breach screening either, so a user can pick a password that is already in every credential-stuffing list.\n\nFix: enforce length-first, machine-guessability-second requirements at registration, screen against a breach/top-passwords list, and never cap below modern passphrase lengths. Keep the existing hashability check — it prevents the PBEKeySpec encoding trap — but make it a precondition, not the policy.\n```java\n// corrected\n    private static final int PASSWORD_MIN_CODEPOINTS = 14;\n    private static final Set<String> COMMON_SECRETS = TopPasswords.load();   // bundled list\n\n    private void requireAcceptablePassword(char[] password) {\n        if (!isHashablePassword(password)) {                       // encoding trap guard, keep it\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password cannot be hashed safely\");\n        }\n        int codePoints = new String(password).codePointCount(0, password.length);\n        if (codePoints < PASSWORD_MIN_CODEPOINTS) {                // length is the cheapest strength\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password must be at least \"\n                + PASSWORD_MIN_CODEPOINTS + \" characters\");\n        }\n        if (COMMON_SECRETS.contains(normalizeForCompare(password))) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"password is too common\");\n        }\n        // In a deployment: check the SHA-1 prefix against a breached-password range API\n        // before accepting, and reject the whole range on API failure (fail closed).\n    }\n```\ncalled from `register()` in place of the bare `isHashablePassword` check, plus: return a machine-readable reason so the UI can say *why*, measure strength in bits (length + character-class diversity + dictionary position) rather than imposing composition rules, and apply the same policy on password change — with an \"allow any printable secret up to 1024 characters, no composition rules, screen against breaches\" posture matching NIST SP 800-63B.\n\n### 6. Verified Controls and Checks With No Finding\n\nBullets with evidence:\n\n- **Constant-time key comparison.** `matches()` compares derived keys with `MessageDigest.isEqual` (`UserAuthentication.java:755-757`) rather than `Arrays.equals` or `ByteBuffer.equals`, so the digest comparison does not leak a prefix length through timing. Caveat recorded as a test gap rather than a vulnerability: the mutation harness reports `non-constant-time-key-compare` SURVIVED (31 mutants, 29 killed, `mut.log:18`, `mut.log:33-35`), i.e. AuthTest's timing check is too coarse to detect the substitution, so this control is currently unprotected against regression.\n- **Equal-cost path for unknown users.** All four branches of `authenticate()` derive exactly one PBKDF2 key before any decision: `476`-`478` either derives the candidate against the stored salt or calls `equalWorkHash.deriveKey(EQUAL_WORK_SECRET)` against the fixed internal digest built at construction (`117`, `EQUAL_WORK_SECRET` `99`-`100`). `mut.log:2` (`equal-work-charge-removed` KILLED) confirms the suite detects removal of the charge. This closes the timing side-channel for account enumeration (CWE-208) on the login path.\n- **Audit-log injection (CWE-117) is closed.** `sanitizeField` (`645`-`660`) restricts every logged field to printable ASCII, drops the record delimiter `|`, replaces control characters (including CR/LF, and thus CRLF injection) with `.`, truncates at 64 characters with a `~` marker, and maps null/empty to `-`. It is applied to username (`269`), source address (`272`) and detail (`275`); the refuse path sanitizes the raw username too (`564`-`569`), so even the `INVALID_INPUT` path cannot smuggle a line. `main` demonstrates it with `mallory\\r\\n|event=LOGIN_SUCCESS|user=admin` (`983`-`984`), and `mut.log:28-29` shows both sanitizer mutants killed.\n- **No stored-record parser malleability.** `parse` (`701`-`718`) pins the prefix, requires exactly four fields, and `strictBase64` (`720`-`734`) re-encodes each decoded field and requires byte-for-byte equality, so non-canonical base64 (which would let two textual records decode to the same key, and which tolerant decoders accept) is rejected (`mut.log:14` killed). Record fields are re-validated for salt/key length ranges (`678`-`683`).\n- **Defensive copies around key material.** The record constructor clones salt and key (`685`-`686`), `salt()` returns a clone (`746`-`748`), and `encode()` emits only the salt (`739`-`741`) — no accessor or `toString` can leak the digest. `mut.log:11-13` shows the three clone mutants killed.\n- **Redacted string representations.** `PasswordHash.toString()` returns `PasswordHash[redacted]` (`774`-`777`) and `Result.toString()` prints outcome/reason/username/duration/attempts only (`246`-`251`); neither can leak a password or a derived key through accidental logging or an exception message (CWE-532).\n- **Encoding-collision guard on passwords.** `isHashablePassword` (`623`-`642`) rejects empty, >1024, trailing-NUL and unpaired-surrogate secrets before `PBEKeySpec` sees them, which closes the known trap where `PBEKeySpec` silently replaces lone surrogates / normalizes encoding so that two different `char[]` values derive the same key (a silent multi-user authentication equivalence). All four guard mutants are killed (`mut.log:6-10`).\n- **Race-free registration and lockout accounting.** `InMemoryAccountStore.register` uses `putIfAbsent` (`805`), so concurrent duplicate registration cannot both succeed (no CWE-367 TOCTOU). `lockStateAt` (`876`-`889`), `recordFailure` (`892`-`904`), `recordSuccess` (`913`) and `forceUnlock` (`922`) are `synchronized` on the `Account`, so the failure-window reset, lock trip and counter clear are consistent under concurrency; the window/lock arithmetic mutants are killed (`mut.log:19-23`).\n- **Audited administrative actions.** `setEnabled` (`534`-`543`) and `unlock` (`546`-`556`) emit `ACCOUNT_ENABLED`/`ACCOUNT_DISABLED`/`ACCOUNT_UNLOCKED` events, so the paths that bypass the lockout leave a trail.\n- **Unicode-canonical username normalization.** `normalizeUsername` (`594`-`612`) applies NFKC, lowercases, then re-checks the `[A-Za-z0-9._-]` allow-list and length (`582`-`591`), so homoglyph/full-width variants cannot mint a distinct-but-colliding account name; the trim/min/max mutants are killed (`mut.log:3-5`).\n- **Salt generation.** `generate` (`689`-`693`) draws a fresh 16-byte salt from the injected `SecureRandom` per password — no fixed salt, no reuse across accounts, salt stored with the record.\n- **No injection sinks anywhere in the component.** Searches for `Runtime.`/`ProcessBuilder`, JDBC (`Statement`, `ResultSet`), `ObjectInputStream`, `javax.script`/`eval(`, `HttpURLConnection`/`Socket` in `UserAuthentication.java` return no matches; the only reflection-style call is the intended `SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(KDF)` (`765`) with a constant algorithm name. There is therefore no reachable sink for OS command injection (CWE-78), SQL injection (CWE-89), unsafe deserialization (CWE-502), expression/template injection (CWE-94/917) or XSS (no HTML rendering at all). `MessageDigest.isEqual` and `Normalizer.normalize` are the only other JDK entry points used.\n- **Test harness safety.** `AuthTest.java` writes only to `Files.createTempFile` paths and deletes them (e.g. `541`-`546`, `810`-`815`) and points the auditor at `\"/private/tmp/auth-test-audit.log\"` in a `FileStore` test rather than the production default; it never opens a socket or spawns a process.\n- **`mutation_test.py` does not itself introduce command injection.** Mutants are built in a fresh `tempfile.mkdtemp()` tree with `subprocess.run([...], shell=False)` argument lists and cleaned with `shutil.rmtree` — no shell interpolation of mutated source text (CWE-78 would otherwise apply to a `shell=True` + string-concatenation harness).\n- **Documentation honesty.** README states the intended posture in terms the code mostly matches (PBKDF2 default 120 000, equal-work design, sanitizer rules, lockout parameters), and flags rate limiting as a complementary control — the gap is called out in SEC-07 rather than misrepresented as implemented.\n- **Clock-injection design note (not a finding).** `Config.clock` (`137`-`155`) makes lockout timing deterministic and testable, but `Event` timestamps (`267`) and `Account.createdAt` (`835`) call `Instant.now()` directly, so audit timestamps and the 30-day inactivity rule do not follow an injected clock. Harmless for security; worth aligning for testability and for consistency if the audit trail is ever used as evidence.\n\nThat's comprehensive.
